| Identifiers |  |

*How COVID‑19 vaccines work.* The video shows the process of vaccination, from injection with RNA or viral vector vaccines, to uptake and translation, and on to immune system stimulation and effect.

A **COVID‑19 vaccine** is designed to induce immunity against [SARS-CoV-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2), the virus responsible for [coronavirus disease 2019](63030231.md) (COVID-19).[^cite_note-BritannicaCOVID-1] COVID-19 vaccines help reduce the risk of severe illness, hospitalization and death from the virus.[^cite_note-2][^cite_note-3]

COVID‑19 vaccines were developed at an unprecedented pace to tackle the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), with the first [clinical trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinical_trial) beginning in March 2020.[^cite_note-thanh-4] Before approval, vaccines underwent the standard three [phases of clinical trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phases_of_clinical_research), although phases were conducted in parallel to accelerate development.[^cite_note-VaccineKnowledgeCOVID-5] Vaccines have been developed based on both classical technologies (using [inactivated virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inactivated_vaccine) or [protein subunit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subunit_vaccine)) and novel platforms ([mRNA](63136688.md) or [viral vector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viral_vector_vaccine)-based).[^cite_note-6]

Prior research on [coronaviruses](201983.md) causing [severe acute respiratory syndrome](196741.md) (SARS) and [Middle East respiratory syndrome](39532251.md) (MERS) accelerated the development of various [vaccine platforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_platform) in early 2020.[^cite_note-pmid33341119-7] The 2023 [Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine](52502.md) was awarded to [Katalin Karikó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katalin_Karik%C3%B3) and [Drew Weissman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drew_Weissman) for the development of effective mRNA vaccines against COVID‑19.[^cite_note-8][^cite_note-9][^cite_note-10]

Major vaccines include the [Pfizer–BioNTech mRNA vaccine](65797150.md), [Moderna mRNA vaccine](63399660.md), and the [Novavax protein subunit vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novavax_COVID-19_vaccine).[^cite_note-VaccineKnowledgeCOVID-5] With the emergence of new [SARS-CoV-2 variants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variants_of_SARS-CoV-2), the original vaccines—particularly [Pfizer](62304.md)–[BioNTech](64671486.md) and [Moderna](39743243.md) vaccines—have been updated. These "variant-adapted" vaccines are offered as [booster doses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Booster_dose).[^cite_note-VaccineKnowledgeCOVID-5] The immunity from the vaccines also wanes over time, requiring people to get boosters to maintain protection.[^cite_note-BritannicaCOVID-1]

Common side effects of COVID‑19 vaccines include soreness, fatigue, headache, [myalgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myalgia) (muscle pain), and [arthralgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthralgia) (joint pain), which resolve without medical treatment within a few days.[^cite_note-COVID-19_vaccine_WHO-11] COVID‑19 vaccination is safe for people who are pregnant or are breastfeeding.[^cite_note-breastf-12]

The COVID‑19 vaccines are widely credited for their role in reducing the spread of COVID‑19 and reducing the severity and death caused by COVID‑19.[^cite_note-BritannicaCOVID-1][^cite_note-13] The Australian-based [medical journal](29500858.md) *[Journal of Paediatrics and Child Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_Paediatrics_and_Child_Health)* estimated that between 14.4 and 19.8 million deaths were prevented by the vaccine.[^cite_note-14] Many countries implemented phased distribution plans that prioritized those at highest risk of complications, such as the elderly, and those at high risk of exposure, such as [healthcare workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_professional).[^cite_note-Beaumont-15][^cite_note-Wang1-16] By December 2020, more than 10 billion vaccine doses had been preordered,[^cite_note-mullard-17] with about half of the doses purchased by [high-income countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank_high-income_economy) comprising 14% of the world's population.[^cite_note-So-18] As of August 2024, over 13 billion doses of COVID‑19 vaccines have been administered worldwide.[^cite_note-Richie_2021-19]

## Background

Before COVID‑19, a vaccine for an infectious disease had never been produced in less than several years – and no vaccine existed for preventing a [coronavirus](201983.md) infection in humans.[^cite_note-gates2-20] However, vaccines have been produced against several animal diseases caused by coronaviruses, including [infectious bronchitis virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infectious_bronchitis_virus) in birds, [canine coronavirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canine_coronavirus), and [feline coronavirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feline_coronavirus).[^cite_note-21]

Previous projects to develop vaccines for viruses in the family *[Coronaviridae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronaviridae)* that affect humans have been aimed at [severe acute respiratory syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severe_acute_respiratory_syndrome) (SARS) and [Middle East respiratory syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_respiratory_syndrome) (MERS). Vaccines against SARS[^cite_note-22] and MERS[^cite_note-23] have been tested in non-human [animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Model_organism).&#x20;

According to studies published in 2005 and 2006, the identification and development of novel vaccines and medicines to treat SARS were priorities for governments and [public health agencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_public_health_agencies) worldwide at the time.[^cite_note-PMID_15655773-24][^cite_note-PMID_15885812-25][^cite_note-PMID_16453264-26] There is no cure or vaccine proven to be safe and effective against SARS in humans.[^cite_note-JiangFutureVirology-27][^cite_note-28] There is also no proven vaccine against MERS.[^cite_note-29] When MERS became prevalent, it was believed that previous SARS research might provide a useful template for developing MERS vaccines.[^cite_note-JiangFutureVirology-27][^cite_note-Butler3oct2013-30] As of March 2020, there was one (DNA-based) MERS vaccine that completed Phase I clinical trials in humans,[^cite_note-31] and three others in progress, all being viral-vectored vaccines: two based on [adenoviruses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adenoviridae) and one on [MVA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modified_vaccinia_Ankara).[^cite_note-32]

Vaccines that use an inactive or weakened virus that has been grown in eggs typically take more than a decade to develop.[^cite_note-Moderna_and_Pfizer_Are_Reinventing_Vaccines-33][^cite_note-34] In contrast, mRNA is a molecule that can be made quickly, and research on mRNA to fight diseases was begun decades before the COVID‑19 pandemic by scientists such as [Drew Weissman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drew_Weissman) and [Katalin Karikó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katalin_Karik%C3%B3), who tested on mice. [Moderna](39743243.md) began human testing of an [mRNA vaccine](63136688.md) in 2015.[^cite_note-Moderna_and_Pfizer_Are_Reinventing_Vaccines-33] [Viral vector vaccines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viral_vector_vaccine) were also developed for the COVID‑19 pandemic after the technology was previously cleared for [Ebola](40817590.md).[^cite_note-Moderna_and_Pfizer_Are_Reinventing_Vaccines-33]

## Vaccine technologies



A conceptual diagram showing three vaccine types for forming SARS‑CoV‑2 proteins to prompt an immune response: (1) [RNA vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNA_vaccine); (2) [subunit vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subunit_vaccine); (3) [viral vector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viral_vector) vaccine



Vaccine platforms that are being employed for SARS-CoV-2. Whole-virus vaccines include both [attenuated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attenuated_vaccine) and [inactivated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inactivated_vaccine) forms of the virus. Protein and [peptide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peptide_vaccine) [subunit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subunit_vaccine) vaccines are usually combined with an [adjuvant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunologic_adjuvant) to enhance immunogenicity. The main emphasis in SARS-CoV-2 vaccine development has been on using the whole [spike protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronavirus_spike_protein) in its trimeric form, or its components, such as the [RBD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binding_domain) region. Multiple non-replicating [viral vector vaccines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viral_vector_vaccine) have been developed, particularly focused on [adenovirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adenoviridae), while there has been less emphasis on the replicating viral vector constructs.

[^cite_note-35]

&#x20;The initial focus of SARS-CoV-2 vaccines was on preventing symptomatic, often severe, illness.[^cite_note-36] Most of the first COVID‑19 vaccines were two-dose vaccines. Exceptions were the single-dose vaccines [Convidecia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convidecia)[^cite_note-WHO_-_Convidecia-37] and the [Janssen COVID‑19 vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janssen_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine),[^cite_note-BritannicaCOVID-1] and vaccines with three-dose schedules, [Razi Cov Pars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Razi_Cov_Pars)[^cite_note-Razi_Cov_Pars_phase_1_trial-38] and [Soberana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soberana_Plus).[^cite_note-Soberana_phase_1/2_trial-39]

In July 2021, at least nine different technology platforms were under research and development to create an effective vaccine against COVID‑19.[^cite_note-thanh22-40][^cite_note-london-41] The [coronavirus spike protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronavirus_spike_protein) (S protein), which the virus uses to enter the cell, induces a strong immune response and is therefore the primary target of vaccines.[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-thanh22-40]

However, other coronavirus proteins were also being investigated for vaccine development, for example, the [nucleocapsid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleocapsid) proteins, because they also induce a robust T-cell response, while their genes are more conserved and recombine less frequently.[^cite_note-Grifoni_2020-43][^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45] Future generations of COVID‑19 vaccines targeting more conserved genomic regions could be used to treat future variations of SARS-CoV-2, or any similar coronavirus epidemic/pandemic.[^cite_note-46]

Platforms developed in 2020 involved [nucleic acid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleic_acid) technologies ([mRNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MRNA) and [DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA)), non-replicating [viral vectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viral_vector), [peptides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peptide), [recombinant proteins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recombinant_protein), live [attenuated viruses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attenuated_virus), and [inactivated viruses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virus_processing#Viral_inactivation).[^cite_note-thanh22-40][^cite_note-thanh-4][^cite_note-diamond-47]

Many vaccine technologies developed for COVID‑19 use "next-generation" strategies for precise targeting of COVID‑19 infection mechanisms.[^cite_note-thanh22-40][^cite_note-thanh-4][^cite_note-diamond-47] Several of the synthetic vaccines use a 2P mutation to lock the [spike protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronavirus_spike_protein) into its prefusion configuration, stimulating an [adaptive immune response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adaptive_immune_response) to the virus before it attaches to a human cell.[^cite_note-48]

### mRNA vaccines

Further information: [mRNA vaccine](63136688.md)



Diagram of the operation of an [RNA vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNA_vaccine). [Messenger RNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messenger_RNA) contained in the vaccine enters cells and is [translated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Translation_\(biology\)) into viral proteins, which trigger an immune response.

Several COVID‑19 vaccines, such as the [Pfizer–BioNTech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pfizer%E2%80%93BioNTech_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine) and [Moderna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moderna_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine) vaccines, use [RNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNA) to stimulate an immune response. When introduced into human tissue, the vaccine contains [messenger RNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messenger_RNA) (mRNA), which causes cells to express the SARS-CoV-2 [spike protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spike_protein). This teaches the [immune system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immune_system) to identify and destroy the corresponding pathogen. RNA vaccines often use [nucleoside-modified messenger RNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleoside-modified_messenger_RNA). The [delivery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug_delivery) of mRNA is achieved by encapsulating the molecule in [lipid nanoparticles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lipid_nanoparticle), which protect the RNA strands and help their uptake into the cells.[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-pmid33340620-50][^cite_note-Kowalski-51][^cite_note-Verbeke_2019-52]

mRNA vaccines were the first COVID‑19 vaccines authorised in the United Kingdom, the United States, and the European Union.[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54] Authorized vaccines of this type include the Pfizer–BioNTech[^cite_note-hcreg-55][^cite_note-NCT04368728-56][^cite_note-EudraCT-2020-001038-36-57] and Moderna vaccines.[^cite_note-NCT04470427-58][^cite_note-palca-59] The [CVnCoV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CVnCoV) mRNA vaccine from [CureVac](39161114.md) failed in clinical trials.[^cite_note-60]

Severe allergic reactions to the mRNA vaccines are rare. In December 2020, 1,893,360 first doses of Pfizer–BioNTech COVID‑19 vaccine administration resulted in 175 cases of severe allergic reactions, of which 21 were [anaphylaxis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaphylaxis).[^cite_note-pmid33571463-61] For 4,041,396 Moderna COVID‑19 vaccine dose administrations in December 2020 and January 2021, only ten cases of anaphylaxis were reported.[^cite_note-pmid33571463-61] [Lipid nanoparticles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid_lipid_nanoparticle) (LNPs) were most likely responsible for the allergic reactions.[^cite_note-pmid33571463-61]

In May 2025, a different version of the Moderna COVID-19 vaccine, with the brand name Mnexspike (mRNA-1283), was approved for medical use in the United States.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64]

In June 2025, the [European Medicines Agency](1427054.md) announced that it was making data from the marketing authorization applications for COVID‑19 mRNA vaccines [Comirnaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comirnaty) and [Spikevax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spikevax) publicly available.[^cite_note-EMA_Transparency-65][^cite_note-Comirnaty_EPAR-66][^cite_note-Spikevax_EPAR-67]

### Viral vector vaccines

Viral vector vaccines use a modified version of a virus to generate immunity. For COVID-19, these vaccines were based on a modified [adenovirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adenoviridae), which normally causes the [common cold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_cold). It is first made harmless, by removing the genes that cause illness and those responsible for replication. The virus is further modified so that it contains DNA that encodes a SARS‑CoV‑2 protein.[^cite_note-vv-gavi-68][^cite_note-69] As such, the virus in the vaccine does not make new adenovirus copies, but only produces the antigen that elicits a systemic immune response.[^cite_note-vv-gavi-68]

Unlike mRNA vaccines, which usually require freezer storage, DNA vaccines such as adenovirus vector vaccines can be kept in a fridge.[^cite_note-Pardi2018mRNAvsDNA-70]

Authorized [vaccines of this type](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viral_vector#Vaccines) include the [Oxford–AstraZeneca COVID‑19 vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford%E2%80%93AstraZeneca_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine),[^cite_note-NCT04400838-71][^cite_note-EudraCT-2020-001228-32-72][^cite_note-ISRCTN89951424-73] the [Sputnik V COVID‑19 vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_V_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine),[^cite_note-sputnik-74] [Convidecia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convidecia), and the [Janssen COVID‑19 vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janssen_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine).[^cite_note-NCT04436276-75][^cite_note-NCT04505722-76]

[Sputnik V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_V_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine) uses Ad26 for its first dose, which is the same as Janssen's only dose, and Ad5 for the second dose, which is the same as Convidecia's only dose.[^cite_note-77] In August 2021, the [developers of Sputnik V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Direct_Investment_Fund) proposed, in view of the Delta case surge, that Pfizer test the Ad26 component (termed its 'Light' version)[^cite_note-78] as a booster shot.[^cite_note-79]

### Inactivated virus vaccines

[Inactivated vaccines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inactivated_vaccine) consist of virus particles grown in [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microbiological_culture) and then inactivated by methods such as heat or [formaldehyde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formaldehyde), thereby losing their disease-producing capacity while still stimulating an immune response.[^cite_note-PetrovskyAguilar2004-80]

Inactivated virus COVID-19 vaccines are less efficient than mRNA vaccines, because the process of de-activating the virus causes the spike protein to change slightly, whereas mRNA vaccines show the spike protein in its normal state. This makes parts of the protein inaccessible to the immune system. The immunity does not last as long as with mRNA vaccines, so that additional boosters are required to maintain immunity.[^cite_note-81]

Inactivated virus vaccines authorized in China include the Chinese [CoronaVac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CoronaVac)[^cite_note-NCT04383574-82][^cite_note-NCname=_nct04456595=-83][^cite_note-PT_Bio_Farma-84] and the Sinopharm [BIBP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinopharm_BIBP_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine)[^cite_note-ChiCTR2000034780-85] and [WIBP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinopharm_WIBP_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine) vaccines; there is also the Indian [Covaxin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBV152), the Russian [CoviVac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CoviVac_\(Russia_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine\)),[^cite_note-86] the [Kazakh](16642.md) vaccine [QazVac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QazVac),[^cite_note-87] and the Iranian [COVIran Barekat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVIran_Barekat).[^cite_note-88]

### Subunit vaccines



Vial of Novavax vaccine from clinical trials at [Thackray Museum of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thackray_Museum_of_Medicine)

[^cite_note-89]

[Subunit vaccines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subunit_vaccine) present one or more [antigens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antigen) without introducing whole pathogen particles. The antigens involved are often [protein subunits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protein_subunit), but they can be any pathogen-derived molecule or its fragment.[^cite_note-90]

The authorized vaccines of this type include the [peptide vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peptide_vaccine) [ZF2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ZF2001),[^cite_note-london-41] [MVC-COV1901](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MVC_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine),[^cite_note-91] [Corbevax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corbevax),[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93] the [Sanofi–GSK vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanofi%E2%80%93GSK_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine),[^cite_note-VidPrevtyn_Beta_EPAR-94][^cite_note-EMA_PR_20221110-95] and [Soberana 02](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soberana_02) (a [conjugate vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conjugate_vaccine)).[^cite_note-96] [Bimervax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bimervax) (selvacovatein) was approved for use as a booster vaccine in the European Union in March 2023.[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98][^cite_note-99] The [Novavax COVID‑19 vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novavax_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine) is another subunit vaccine. This vaccine is produced by infecting moth cells with a modified [baculovirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baculoviridae) vector, which contains a gene for a [SARS-CoV-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severe_acute_respiratory_syndrome_coronavirus_2) [spike protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronavirus_spike_protein).[^cite_note-Hotez-100]

The clinical trials of [V451 vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V451_vaccine) were terminated after it was found that the vaccine may potentially cause incorrect results for subsequent HIV testing.[^cite_note-NCT04495933-101][^cite_note-dwuq-102][^cite_note-103]

[Virus-like particle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virus-like_particle) (VLP) vaccines are one type of subunit vaccines. They look similar to viruses, but do not contain any RNA or DNA, and are non-replicating. They can be stored in a fridge, rather than using extreme cold like mRNA vaccines. One such vaccine, [Covifenz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covifenz), was authorized for use in 2022 in Canada, but later withdrawn.[^cite_note-104]

### Other types

Additional types of vaccines that are in clinical trials include multiple [DNA plasmid vaccines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_vaccine),[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-NCT04336410-106][^cite_note-107][^cite_note-NCT04463472-108][^cite_note-NCT04445389-109][^cite_note-gen-reut-110] at least two [lentivirus vector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lentiviral_vector_in_gene_therapy) vaccines,[^cite_note-NCT04299724-111][^cite_note-NCT04276896-112] a [conjugate vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conjugate_vaccine), and a [vesicular stomatitis virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vesicular_stomatitis_virus) displaying the SARS‑CoV‑2 spike protein.[^cite_note-NCT04608305-113]

Scientists investigated whether existing vaccines for unrelated conditions could prime the immune system and lessen the severity of COVID‑19 infections.[^cite_note-114] There is experimental evidence that the [BCG vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BCG_vaccine) for tuberculosis has non-specific effects on the immune system, but clinical trials showed this vaccine is not effective against COVID‑19.[^cite_note-115]

### Formulation

An [immunological adjuvant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunological_adjuvant) is a substance formulated with a vaccine to elevate the immune response to an [antigen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antigen), such as the COVID‑19 virus or influenza virus.[^cite_note-tregoning-116] Adjuvants used in COVID‑19 vaccine formulations may be particularly effective for technologies using the inactivated COVID‑19 virus and recombinant protein-based or vector-based vaccines.[^cite_note-wang-117]

## Authorized vaccines

Main article: [List of COVID-19 vaccine authorizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_COVID-19_vaccine_authorizations)



Map of countries by approval status of COVID‑19 vaccines (2021)&#x20;

&#x20; Approved for general use, mass vaccination underway

&#x20; [EUA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_Use_Authorization) (or equivalent) granted, mass vaccination underway

&#x20; EUA granted, mass vaccination planned

&#x20; No data available

By the end of 2022, 50 COVID-19 vaccines had received authorization in one or more countries—meaning they were approved, licensed, or granted emergency use authorization by national regulatory agencies around the world.[^cite_note-TrackVaccinesApproved-118][^cite_note-Pormohammad2021-119]

The [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) monitors circulating strains and the effectiveness of different updated vaccines against them. As of May 2025, the WHO recommends that vaccines targeting [JN.1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BA.2.86) or KP.2 remain appropriate and that LP.8.1 is a suitable alternative vaccine antigen.[^cite_note-120] The [European Medicines Agency](1427054.md) recommends updating COVID-19 vaccines to target LP.8.1 for the 2025/2026 vaccination campaign.[^cite_note-121] At the same time, the FDA in the US advised manufacturers that the COVID-19 vaccines beginning in fall 2025 should be JN.1-lineage-based COVID-19 vaccines, preferentially using the LP.8.1 strain.[^cite_note-122]

### Combination vaccines

A [combined messenger RNA vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Influenza_and_COVID-19_vaccine) for protection against [influenza](19572217.md) and COVID‑19 was introduced in 2026.[^cite_note-Mcombriax_EPAR-123][^cite_note-EMA_PR_2026027-124]

## Delivery methods



A US [airman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airman) receiving a COVID‑19 vaccine, December 2020

Most coronavirus vaccines are administered by [intramuscular injection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuscular_injection), with further vaccine delivery methods being studied for future coronavirus vaccines.

### Intranasal

Intranasal vaccines target [mucosal immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mucosal_immunology) in the [nasal mucosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasal_mucosa), which is a portal for viral entry into the body.[^cite_note-mudgal-125][^cite_note-rhee-126] These vaccines are designed to stimulate nasal [immune factors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immune_factor), such as [IgA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunoglobulin_A).[^cite_note-mudgal-125] In addition to inhibiting the virus, nasal vaccines provide ease of administration because no needles (or [needle phobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fear_of_needles)) are involved.[^cite_note-rhee-126][^cite_note-cdc-127]

A variety of intranasal COVID‑19 vaccines are undergoing clinical trials. The first authorized intranasal vaccine was [Razi Cov Pars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Razi_Cov_Pars) in Iran at the end of October 2021.[^cite_note-Tehran_Times-128] The first viral component of [Sputnik V vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_V_COVID-19_vaccine) was authorized in Russia as Sputnik Nasal in April 2022.[^cite_note-Sibmeda-129] In September 2022, India and China approved two [nasal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasal_vaccine) COVID‑19 vaccines ([iNCOVACC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INCOVACC) and [Convidecia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convidecia)), which may (as boosters)[^cite_note-130] also reduce transmission[^cite_note-naturenasalvac-131][^cite_note-132] (potentially via sterilizing immunity).[^cite_note-naturenasalvac-131] In December 2022, China approved a second intranasal vaccine as a booster, brand name [Pneucolin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DelNS1-2019-nCoV-RBD-OPT).[^cite_note-Pneucolin-133]

## Universal vaccine

Main article: [Universal coronavirus vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_coronavirus_vaccine)

A universal coronavirus vaccine would be effective against all coronaviruses and possibly other viruses.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-Joi_2021-135] The concept was publicly endorsed by [NIAID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NIAID) director [Anthony Fauci](1227250.md), [virologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virology) [Jeffery K. Taubenberger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffery_Taubenberger), and David M. Morens.[^cite_note-Bush_2021-136] In March 2022, the [White House](33057.md) released the "National COVID‑19 Preparedness Plan", which recommended accelerating the development of a universal coronavirus vaccine.[^cite_note-137]

One attempt at such a vaccine is being developed at the [Walter Reed Army Institute of Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Reed_Army_Institute_of_Research). It uses a spike ferritin-based nanoparticle (SpFN). This vaccine began a Phase I clinical trial in April 2022.[^cite_note-138] Results of this trial were published in May 2024.[^cite_note-WRAIR_phase_1_trial-139] Other universal vaccines that have entered clinical trial include OVX033 (France),[^cite_note-OVX033_phase_1_trial-140] PanCov (France),[^cite_note-PanCov_phase_1/2_trial-141] pEVAC-PS (UK),[^cite_note-Diosynvax_phase_1-142] and [VBI-2902](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VBI-2902) (Canada).[^cite_note-VBI_phase_1_trial-143]

Another strategy is to attach vaccine fragments from multiple strains to a [nanoparticle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanoparticle) scaffold. One theory is that a broader range of strains can be vaccinated against by targeting the receptor-binding domain, rather than the whole [spike protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spike_protein).[^cite_note-144]

## Development

This section is an excerpt from [History of COVID-19 vaccine development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development\&action=edit)]



COVID‑19 vaccine research samples in a [NIAID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NIAID) lab freezer (30 January 2020)

[SARS-CoV-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severe_acute_respiratory_syndrome_coronavirus_2), the virus that causes [COVID-19](63030231.md), was isolated in late 2019.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_who-time-145] Its [genetic sequence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleic_acid_sequence) was published on 11 January 2020, setting off an intense research effort to develop a COVID-19 vaccine.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_thanh-146][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_gates1-147][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_Fauci-148] Vaccine development was expedited via unprecedented collaboration between [academia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_institution), the [pharmaceutical industry](560876.md) and governments.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_thanh2-149] By June 2020, tens of billions of dollars were being invested to develop dozens of vaccine candidates and prepare for global vaccination programs.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_gates1-147][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_hbr-150][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_cbcr-151][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_ahmed-152] According to the [Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations](51522180.md) (CEPI), the geographic distribution of COVID‑19 vaccine development shows North American entities to have about 40% of the activity, compared to 30% in Asia and Australia, 26% in Europe, and a few projects in South America and Africa.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_thanh-146][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_thanh2-149]

In February 2020, the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) said it did not expect a vaccine against SARS‑CoV‑2 to become available in less than 18 months.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_\:0-153] Virologist Paul Offit commented that, in hindsight, the development of a safe and effective vaccine within 11 months was a remarkable feat.[^cite_note-154] The rapidly growing infection rate of COVID‑19 worldwide during 2020 stimulated international alliances and government efforts to urgently organize resources to make multiple vaccines on shortened timelines;[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_who-accel-155] Four vaccine candidates entered human evaluation in March.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_thanh-146][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_yamey-156]

On 24 June 2020, China approved the CanSino vaccine for limited use in the military and two inactivated virus vaccines for emergency use in high-risk occupations.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_\:1-157] On 11 August 2020, Russia announced the approval of its Sputnik V vaccine for emergency use, though one month later only small amounts of the vaccine had been distributed for use outside of the phase 3 trial.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_\:2-158]

The Pfizer–BioNTech partnership submitted an [Emergency Use Authorization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_Use_Authorization) (EUA) request to the U.S. [Food and Drug Administration](11632.md) (FDA) for their mRNA vaccine on 20 November 2020.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_\:3-159][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_\:4-160] On 2 December 2020, the United Kingdom's [Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicines_and_Healthcare_products_Regulatory_Agency) (MHRA) gave temporary regulatory approval for the [Pfizer–BioNTech vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pfizer%E2%80%93BioNTech_COVID%E2%80%9119_vaccine),[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_MHRA_2020-12-08-161][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_\:5-162] becoming the first country to approve the vaccine and the first country in the [Western world](21208200.md) to approve the use of any COVID‑19 vaccine.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_ukgov12-2-163][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_mueller-164][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_\:6-165] The US and the EU followed later that month.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_eu_vac-166][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_thomas-167] The US also granted an EUA for the [Moderna COVID-19 vaccine](63399660.md) in December.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_CDC_ACIP_Moderna_COVID-19_Vaccine-168][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_CNBC_20201219-169] [Bahrain](18933277.md) and the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) granted emergency marketing authorization for the [Sinopharm BIBP vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinopharm_BIBP_COVID-19_vaccine).[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_bna-170][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_uae-171]

On 31 March 2021, the Russian government announced that they had registered the first COVID‑19 vaccine for animals.[^cite_note-172] Named [Carnivac-Cov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnivac-Cov), it is an inactivated vaccine for carnivorous animals, including pets, aimed at preventing mutations that occur during the interspecies transmission of SARS-CoV-2.[^cite_note-173]

In October 2022, China began administering an oral vaccine developed by [CanSino Biologics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CanSino_Biologics) using its adenovirus model.[^cite_note-174]

Despite the availability of [mRNA](63136688.md) and [viral vector vaccines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viral_vector_vaccine), worldwide [vaccine equity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_equity) has not been achieved. The ongoing development and use of [whole inactivated virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whole_inactivated_virus) (WIV) and [protein-based](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subunit_vaccine) vaccines has been recommended, especially for use in [developing countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developing_countries), to dampen further waves of the pandemic.[^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_Hotez-175][^cite_note-History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development_Ye-176]

In June 2025, the [European Medicines Agency](1427054.md) announced that it was making data from the marketing authorization applications for COVID‑19 mRNA vaccines [Comirnaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comirnaty) and [Spikevax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spikevax) publicly available.[^cite_note-EMA_Transparency-65][^cite_note-Comirnaty_EPAR-66][^cite_note-Spikevax_EPAR-67]

Multiple steps along the entire development path are evaluated, including:[^cite_note-gates2-20][^cite_note-simpson-177]

- the level of acceptable toxicity of the vaccine (its safety),

- targeting vulnerable populations,

- the need for vaccine efficacy breakthroughs,

- the duration of vaccination protection,

- special delivery systems (such as oral or nasal, rather than by injection),

- dose regimen,

- stability and storage characteristics,

- emergency use authorization before formal licensing,

- optimal manufacturing for scaling to billions of doses, and

- dissemination of the licensed vaccine.

### Challenges

There have been several unique challenges with COVID‑19 vaccine development.

Timelines for conducting clinical research usually a sequential process requiring years – were being compressed into safety, efficacy, and dosing trials running simultaneously over months.[^cite_note-sanger-178][^cite_note-reuters-179] Under normal circumstances, large-scale trials involving thousands of participants only proceed after smaller studies confirm there are no significant safety concerns.[^cite_note-180] For example, Chinese vaccine developers and the [Chinese Center for Disease Control and Prevention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Center_for_Disease_Control_and_Prevention) began their efforts in January 2020,[^cite_note-181] and by March they were pursuing numerous candidates on short timelines.[^cite_note-sanger-178][^cite_note-wee-182]

The rapid development and urgency of producing a vaccine for the COVID‑19 pandemic were expected to increase the risks and failure rate of delivering a safe, effective vaccine.[^cite_note-thanh-4][^cite_note-diamond-47][^cite_note-Thorp-183] Additionally, research at universities is obstructed by [physical distancing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_distancing) and the closing of laboratories.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-185]

Vaccines must progress through several phases of [clinical trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinical_trial) to test for safety, [immunogenicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunogenicity), effectiveness, dose levels, and [adverse effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_adverse_event) of the candidate vaccine.[^cite_note-Vaccines.gov-186][^cite_note-fda-ddp-187] Vaccine developers have to invest resources internationally to find enough participants for Phase II–III clinical trials when the virus has proved to be a "[moving target](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moving_target_indication)" of changing transmission rates across and within countries, forcing companies to compete for trial participants.[^cite_note-cohen6-19-188]

As new vaccines are developed during the COVID‑19 pandemic, licensure of COVID‑19 vaccine candidates requires submission of a full dossier of information on development and manufacturing quality.[^cite_note-ema-eau-189][^cite_note-byrne10-19-190][^cite_note-dangerfield-191]

### Organizations involved in COVID-19 vaccine development

Internationally, the [Access to COVID‑19 Tools Accelerator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Access_to_COVID%E2%80%9119_Tools_Accelerator) is a [G20](2206608.md) and [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) initiative announced in April 2020.[^cite_note-g20gm-192][^cite_note-whopress-193] It is a cross-discipline support structure to enable partners to share resources and knowledge. It comprises four pillars, each managed by two to three collaborating partners: Vaccines (also called "[COVAX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVAX)"), Diagnostics, Therapeutics, and Health Systems Connector.[^cite_note-act-faq-194] The WHO's April 2020 "R\&D Blueprint (for the) novel Coronavirus" documented a "large, international, multi-site, individually randomized controlled clinical trial" to allow "the concurrent evaluation of the benefits and risks of each promising candidate vaccine within 3–6 months of it being made available for the trial." The WHO vaccine coalition will prioritize which vaccines should go into Phase II and III clinical trials and determine harmonized Phase III protocols for all vaccines achieving the [pivotal trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pivotal_trial) stage.[^cite_note-who-accel-195]

National governments have also been involved in vaccine development. Canada announced funding for 96 projects for the development and production of vaccines at Canadian companies and universities, with plans to establish a "vaccine bank" that could be used if another coronavirus outbreak occurs,[^cite_note-abedi-196] support clinical trials, and develop manufacturing and supply chains for vaccines.[^cite_note-can-research-197]

[China](23239.md) provided low-rate loans to one vaccine developer through its [central bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Bank_of_China) and "quickly made land available for the company" to build production plants.[^cite_note-reuters-179] Three Chinese vaccine companies and research institutes are supported by the government for financing research, conducting clinical trials, and manufacturing.[^cite_note-takada-198]

The United Kingdom government formed a COVID‑19 vaccine [task force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Task_force) in April 2020 to stimulate local efforts for accelerated development of a vaccine through collaborations between industries, universities, and government agencies. The UK's [Vaccine Taskforce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_Taskforce) contributed to every phase of development, from research to manufacturing.[^cite_note-morriss-199]

In the United States, the [Biomedical Advanced Research and Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomedical_Advanced_Research_and_Development_Authority) (BARDA), a federal agency funding disease-fighting technology, announced investments to support American COVID‑19 vaccine development and the manufacturing of the most promising candidates.[^cite_note-reuters-179][^cite_note-kuznia-200] In May 2020, the government announced funding for a fast-track program called [Operation Warp Speed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Warp_Speed).[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202] By March 2021, BARDA had funded an estimated \$19.3 billion in COVID‑19 vaccine development.[^cite_note-203]

Large pharmaceutical companies with experience in making vaccines at scale, including [Johnson & Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson_&_Johnson), [AstraZeneca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AstraZeneca), and [GlaxoSmithKline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GlaxoSmithKline) (GSK), formed alliances with [biotechnology](4502.md) companies, governments, and universities to accelerate progress toward effective vaccines.[^cite_note-reuters-179][^cite_note-sanger-178]

## Effectiveness



Death rates from COVID-19 for unvaccinated Americans substantially exceeded those who were vaccinated, with bivalent boosters further reducing the death rate.

[^cite_note-COVIDmortality_2021_2023-204]



Researchers have estimated that COVID-19 vaccines saved around 20 million lives globally in the first year of vaccine availability.

[^cite_note-205]

This section is an excerpt from [COVID-19 vaccine clinical research § Effectiveness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research#Effectiveness).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research\&action=edit)]

Evidence from vaccine use during the pandemic shows vaccination can reduce infection and is most effective at preventing severe COVID-19 symptoms and death, but is less good at preventing mild COVID-19. Efficacy wanes over time but can be maintained with boosters.[^cite_note-COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research_YangJiangLi2023-206] In 2021, the CDC reported that unvaccinated people were 10 times more likely to be hospitalized and 11 times more likely to die than fully vaccinated people.[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208]

The CDC reported that vaccine effectiveness fell from 91% against [Alpha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Alpha_variant) to 66% against Delta.[^cite_note-209] One expert stated that "those who are infected following vaccination are still not getting sick and not dying like was happening before vaccination."[^cite_note-COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research_TNR-210] By late August 2021, the Delta variant accounted for 99 percent of U.S. cases and was found to double the risk of severe illness and hospitalization for those not yet vaccinated.[^cite_note-COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research_LATimes-delta-211]

On 10 December 2021, the [UK Health Security Agency](64959819.md) reported that early data indicated a 20- to 40-fold reduction in neutralizing activity for Omicron by sera from Pfizer 2-dose vaccinees relative to earlier strains. After a booster dose (usually with an mRNA vaccine),[^cite_note-212] vaccine effectiveness against symptomatic disease was at 70%–75%, and the effectiveness against severe disease was expected to be higher.[^cite_note-COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research_briefing31-213]

A meta-analysis looking into COVID-19 vaccine differences in immunosuppressed individuals found that people with a weakened immune system are less able to produce neutralizing antibodies. For example, organ transplant recipients need three vaccines to achieve [seroconversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seroconversion).[^cite_note-214] A study on the serologic response to mRNA vaccines among patients with lymphoma, leukemia, and myeloma found that one-quarter of patients did not produce measurable antibodies, varying by cancer type.[^cite_note-215]

In February 2023, a systematic review in *[The Lancet](463899.md)* said that the protection afforded by infection was comparable to that from vaccination, albeit with an increased risk of severe illness and death from the disease of an initial infection.[^cite_note-COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research_SteinNassereldineSorensen2023-216]

A January 2024 study by the CDC found that staying up to date on the vaccines could reduce the risk of strokes, blood clots and heart attacks related to COVID-19 in people aged 65 years or older or with a condition that makes them more vulnerable to said conditions.[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218]

### Against infection and transmission

Transmission is mostly due to mild infections. Vaccines show lower effectiveness against mild infections than severe infections, and the effectivenss decreases within six months of vaccination.[^cite_note-219] [Vaccine efficacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_efficacy) against infections was around 77% for pre-Omicron variants in the first months after vaccination, and 26% against Omicron variants. The efficacy against infections waned more rapidly than against severe illness. Boosters restored the protection against infections.[^cite_note-220]

#### Duration of immunity

As of 2021, available evidence shows that fully vaccinated individuals and those previously infected with SARS-CoV-2 have a low risk of subsequent infection for at least six months.[^cite_note-CDC_Science_Brief-221][^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223] There is insufficient data to determine an antibody titer threshold that indicates when an individual is protected from infection.[^cite_note-CDC_Science_Brief-221] Multiple studies show that antibody titers are associated with protection at the population level, but individual protection titers remain unknown.[^cite_note-CDC_Science_Brief-221] For some populations, such as the elderly and the [immunocompromised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunocompromised), protection levels may be reduced after both vaccination and infection.[^cite_note-CDC_Science_Brief-221] Available evidence indicates that the level of protection may not be the same for all [variants of the virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variants_of_SARS-CoV-2).[^cite_note-CDC_Science_Brief-221]

As of December 2021, there are no FDA-authorized or approved tests that providers or the public can use to determine if a person is protected from infection reliably.[^cite_note-CDC_Science_Brief-221]

As of March 2022, elderly residents' protection against severe illness, hospitalization, and death in English care homes was high immediately after vaccination, but protection declined significantly in the months following vaccination.[^cite_note-Guardian_20220318-224] Protection among care home staff, who were younger, declined much more slowly.[^cite_note-Guardian_20220318-224] Regular boosters are recommended for older people, and boosters for care home residents every six months appear reasonable.[^cite_note-Guardian_20220318-224]

The US [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention](6811.md) (CDC) recommends a fourth dose of the Pfizer mRNA vaccine as of March 2022 for "certain immunocompromised individuals and people over the age of 50".[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-226]

### Against severe illness and death

This section is an excerpt from [COVID-19 vaccine clinical research § Against severe illness and death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research#Against_severe_illness_and_death).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research\&action=edit)]

Overall, vaccines had a 87%–90% protection against severe outcomes, such as hospitalisation.[^cite_note-COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research_\:0-227][^cite_note-COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research_\:1-228] Six months after vaccination, the protection was still 76%. Like with infections, the protection was stronger against pre-Omicron variants than Omicron.[^cite_note-COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research_\:0-227] Protection against dying from COVID was similar: around 86%.[^cite_note-COVID-19_vaccine_clinical_research_\:1-228]

According to a June 2022 study, COVID‑19 vaccines prevented an additional 14.4 to 19.8 million deaths in 185 countries and territories from 8 December 2020 to 8 December 2021.[^cite_note-229] A 2024 study estimated around 1.6 million lives saves in the [wider European region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_WHO_regions).[^cite_note-230] A study published in 2025 estimated that COVID-19 vaccinations globally averted approximately 2.5 million deaths between 2020 and 2024, with the majority of the benefit concentrated among older adults. These estimates were more conservative than those reported in previous studies, using different estimates of vaccine effectiveness and mortality rates.[^cite_note-231]

### Immune evasion by variants

In contrast to other investigated prior variants, the [SARS-CoV-2 Omicron variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant)[^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233] and its BA.4/5 subvariants[^cite_note-234] have [evaded immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antigenic_escape) induced by vaccines, which may lead to [breakthrough infections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breakthrough_infection) despite recent vaccination. Nevertheless, vaccines are thought to provide protection against severe illness, hospitalizations, and deaths due to Omicron.[^cite_note-235]

#### Vaccine adjustments

This section is an excerpt from [SARS-CoV-2 Omicron variant § Vaccine adjustments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant#Vaccine_adjustments).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant\&action=edit)]

In June 2022, [Pfizer](62304.md) and [Moderna](39743243.md) developed [bivalent vaccines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bivalent_vaccine) to protect against the [SARS-CoV-2 wild-type](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2) and the Omicron variant. The bivalent vaccines are well-tolerated and offer immunity to Omicron superior to previous mRNA vaccines.[^cite_note-SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant_pmid36303305-236] In September 2022, the United States [Food and Drug Administration](11632.md) (FDA) authorized the bivalent vaccines.[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239]

In June 2023, the FDA advised manufacturers that the 2023–2024 formulation of the COVID‑19 vaccines for use in the US be updated to be a monovalent COVID‑19 vaccine using the XBB.1.5 lineage of the Omicron variant.[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241] In June 2024, the FDA advised manufacturers that the 2024–2025 formulation of the COVID‑19 vaccines for use in the US be updated to be a monovalent COVID‑19 vaccine using the JN.1 lineage.[^cite_note-242]

In October 2024, the [Committee for Medicinal Products for Human Use](11686743.md) of the [European Medicines Agency](1427054.md) (EMA) gave a positive opinion to update the composition of Bimervax, a vaccine targeting the Omicron XBB.1.16 subvariant.[^cite_note-243]

In May 2025, the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) recommended that monovalent JN.1 or KP.2 vaccines remain appropriate vaccine antigens and that monovalent LP.8.1 is a suitable alternative vaccine antigen.[^cite_note-244] The EMA recommends updating COVID-19 vaccines to target LP.8.1 for the 2025/2026 vaccination campaign.[^cite_note-245]

In May 2026, the WHO advised vaccine manufacturers that monovalent LP.8.1 (Nextstrain: 25A; GenBank: PV074550.1; GISAID: EPI_ISL_19467828) is the recommended COVID-19 vaccine antigen.[^cite_note-SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant_WHO_20260516-246] Other antigens (e.g. XFG, NB.1.8.1) or other approaches that demonstrate broad and robust neutralizing antibody responses or efficacy against currently circulating SARS-CoV-2 variants could also be used.[^cite_note-SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant_WHO_20260516-246]

In May 2026, the US FDA advised manufacturers that the COVID-19 vaccines (2026-2027 Formula) for use in the United States beginning in fall 2026 should be a monovalent JN.1 lineage XFG variant vaccine.[^cite_note-247]

In May 2026, the EMA suggested that targeting XFG would provide the best protection against COVID-19 caused by JN.1 omicron subvariants, and also against BA.3.2.[^cite_note-SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant_EMA_20260526-248] Vaccines targeting the LP.8.1 strain could still be considered for vaccination campaigns in 2026.[^cite_note-SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant_EMA_20260526-248]

### Against long COVID

COVID-19 vaccination seems to reduce risk of [long COVID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_COVID). Three doses of a COVID-19 vaccine may reduce the risk by 70%, while two doses give 43% protection and one dose gives 19% protection. The evidence is based on observational studies, rather than on randomized clinical trials.[^cite_note-249] The large randomized control trials on COVID-19 vaccines did not include long COVID as an [outcome measure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outcome_measure).[^cite_note-250]

An analysis involving more than 20 million adults found that vaccinated people had a lower risk of long COVID compared with those who had not received a COVID‑19 vaccine; they were also protected against blood clots and heart failure after the COVID infection.[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253]

### Mix and match

According to studies, the combination of two different COVID‑19 vaccines, also called [heterologous vaccination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterologous_vaccine), cross-vaccination, or the mix-and-match method, provides protection equivalent to that of mRNA vaccines, including protection against the [Delta variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_variant). Individuals who receive the combination of two different vaccines produce strong immune responses, with side effects no worse than those caused by standard regimens.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255]

## Adverse effects

The risks of serious illness from COVID-19 are far higher than the risk posed by the vaccines.[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258] For most people, the side effects, also called *[adverse effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adverse_effects)*, from COVID‑19 vaccines are mild and can be managed at home. The adverse effects of the COVID‑19 vaccination are similar to those of other vaccines, and severe adverse effects are rare.[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260] Adverse effects from the vaccine are higher than placebo, but placebo arms of vaccine trials still reported adverse effects that can be attributed to the [nocebo effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nocebo_effect).[^cite_note-261]

A February 2022 study, which examined a potential causal link between COVID-19 vaccines and excess deaths, found that out of more than 250 million [Americans vaccinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_the_United_States), only 55 cases of death after COVID-19 vaccination were reported and in 17 of these, a causal relationship had been excluded.[^cite_note-262] The [Paul Ehrlich Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Ehrlich_Institute) has recorded 31 [cerebral venous sinus thromboses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerebral_venous_sinus_thrombosis) (CVST) and nine deaths out of 2.7 million [vaccinated in Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_Germany) with the [AZD1222](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AZD1222).[^cite_note-Gallagher_2021-263] The UK [Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicines_and_Healthcare_products_Regulatory_Agency) (MHRA) recorded 73 deaths out of nearly 50 million doses of AstraZeneca [given in UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_the_United_Kingdom).[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265]

All vaccines administered via [intramuscular injection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intramuscular_injection), including COVID‑19 vaccines, have side effects related to the mild trauma associated with the procedure and the introduction of a foreign substance into the body.[^cite_note-Polania-266] These include soreness, redness, rash, and inflammation at the injection site. Other common side effects include fatigue, headache, [myalgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myalgia) (muscle pain), and [arthralgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthralgia) (joint pain), all of which generally resolve without medical treatment within a few days.[^cite_note-COVID-19_vaccine_WHO-11] These common side effects result from the body responding to the vaccine to build up protection from the virus.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268]

Like any other vaccine, some people are [allergic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allergic) to one or more ingredients in COVID‑19 vaccines. Typical side effects are stronger and more common in younger people and in subsequent doses, and up to 20% of people report a disruptive level of side effects after the second dose of an mRNA vaccine.[^cite_note-Huang_2022-269] These side effects are less common or weaker in [inactivated vaccines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inactivated_vaccine).[^cite_note-Huang_2022-269] COVID‑19 vaccination-related [enlargement of lymph nodes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lymphadenopathy) happens in 11.6% of those who received one dose of the vaccine and in 16% of those who received two doses.[^cite_note-270]

COVID‑19 vaccination is safe for breastfeeding people.[^cite_note-breastf-12] Temporary changes to the [menstrual cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menstrual_cycle) in young women have been reported. However, these changes are "small compared with natural variation and quickly reverse."[^cite_note-Menstruation-271] In one study, women who received both doses of a two-dose vaccine during the same menstrual cycle (an atypical situation) may see their next period begin a couple of days late. They have about twice the usual risk of a clinically significant delay (about 10% of these women, compared to about 4% of unvaccinated women).[^cite_note-Menstruation-271] Cycle lengths return to normal after two menstrual cycles post-vaccination.[^cite_note-Menstruation-271] Women who received doses in separate cycles had approximately the same natural variation in cycle lengths as unvaccinated women.[^cite_note-Menstruation-271] Other temporary menstrual effects have been reported, such as heavier than normal menstrual bleeding after vaccination.[^cite_note-Menstruation-271]

### Serious adverse events

Serious [adverse events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_adverse_event) associated COVID‑19 vaccines are generally rare but of high interest to the public.[^cite_note-Montgomery_2021-272] The official databases of reported adverse events include:

- the [World Health Organization](33583.md)'s [VigiBase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VigiBase);

- the United States [Vaccine Adverse Events Reporting System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_Adverse_Events_Reporting_System) (VAERS);

- the United Kingdom's [Yellow Card Scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_Card_Scheme);

- the [European Medicines Agency](1427054.md)'s [EudraVigilance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EudraVigilance) system, which operates a regular transfer of data on suspected adverse drug reactions occurring in the EU to [WHO's Uppsala Monitoring Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uppsala_Monitoring_Centre).

  [^cite_note-273]

Increased public awareness of these reporting systems and the extra reporting requirements under US FDA [Emergency Use Authorization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_Use_Authorization) rules have increased reported adverse events.[^cite_note-274] Serious side effects are an ongoing area of study, and resources have been allocated to try to better understand them.[^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277] Research currently indicates that the rate and type of side effects are lower-risk than infection. For example, although vaccination may trigger some side effects, the effects experienced from an infection could be worse. Neurological side effects from getting COVID‑19 are hundreds of times more likely than from vaccination.[^cite_note-278] Evidence suggests that vaccination confers no significantly increased risk of serious adverse effects, including various cardiovascular, metabolic, and endocrine events, based on a cohort study involving 1 million adults in Denmark who have received an mRNA COVID-19 booster shot targeting the Omicron JN.1 variant.[^cite_note-Denmark_neuro_study_boostershot-279]

Documented rare serious effects include:

- [anaphylaxis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaphylaxis), a severe type of [allergic reaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allergic_reaction).

  [^cite_note-Altmann_2022-280]

  &#x20;Anaphylaxis affects one person per 250,000 to 400,000 doses administered.

  [^cite_note-Huang_2022-269]

  [^cite_note-COVID-19_vaccines_safety_update-281]

  &#x20;The rate is in line with the expected rate of anaphylaxis to non-COVID vaccines.

  [^cite_note-282]

  [^cite_note-283]

  [^cite_note-284]

  [^cite_note-285]

  &#x20;According to a 2022 systematic review, the mortality rate of people with anaphylaxis following COVID‐19 vaccination was 0.5%. The review concluded the consensus of the studies was that "the risk of anaphylaxis is not significant when compared to the risks posed by COVID‐19 infection".

  [^cite_note-286]

* [blood clots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrombus) (*thrombosis*).

  [^cite_note-Altmann_2022-280]

  &#x20;These [vaccine-induced immune thrombocytopenia and thrombosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embolic_and_thrombotic_events_after_COVID-19_vaccination) are associated with vaccines using an adenovirus system (Janssen and Oxford-AstraZeneca).

  [^cite_note-Altmann_2022-280]

  &#x20;These affect about one person per 100,000.

  [^cite_note-Huang_2022-269]

  &#x20;There is a far higher risk of developing blood clots as a result of COVID-19 infection than from the vaccines.

  [^cite_note-287]

* [myocarditis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myocarditis) and [pericarditis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pericarditis), or inflammation of the heart.

  [^cite_note-Altmann_2022-280]

  &#x20;There is a rare risk of myocarditis (inflammation of the heart muscle) or pericarditis (inflammation of the membrane covering the heart) after the mRNA COVID‑19 vaccines (Moderna or Pfizer-BioNTech). The risk of myocarditis after COVID‑19 vaccination is estimated to be 0.3 to 5 cases per 100,000 persons, with the highest risk in young males.

  [^cite_note-NEJM_Basso-288]

  &#x20;COVID‑19 vaccines may protect against myocarditis due to subsequent COVID‑19 infection.

  [^cite_note-AHA_8/2022-289]

  [^cite_note-290]

  &#x20;The risk of myocarditis and pericarditis is significantly higher (up to 11 times higher with respect to myocarditis) after COVID‑19 infection as compared to COVID‑19 vaccination, with the possible exception of men under 40 who may have a higher risk of myocarditis after the second Moderna mRNA vaccine (an additional 97 cases of myocarditis per 1 million persons vaccinated).

  [^cite_note-AHA_8/2022-289]

  &#x20;The mortality rate from myocarditis post-vaccination is extremely low. According to a 2022 study, of patients diagnosed with myocarditis (in both vaccination and COVID-19 cohorts), 1.07% were hospitalized and 0.015% died.

  [^cite_note-291]

  &#x20;As of June 2025, the US FDA requires a prescription label warning regarding myocarditis and pericarditis.

  [^cite_note-292]

- thrombotic thrombocytopenia and other autoimmune diseases, which have been reported as adverse events after the COVID‑19 vaccine.

  [^cite_note-293]

* new onset [tinnitus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tinnitus) after the first dose of certain vaccines; however, there is a higher risk of the same symptom developing after influenza and pneumococcal vaccinations than after the COVID-19 vaccine.

  [^cite_note-Slomski_2022-294]

  [^cite_note-295]

As of 12 August 2024, 13.53 billion COVID-19 vaccine doses have been administered worldwide, with 70.6 percent of the global population having received at least one dose.[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308] While 4.19 million vaccines were then being administered daily, only 22.3 percent of people in low-income countries had received at least a first vaccine by September 2022, according to official reports from national health agencies, which are collated by [Our World in Data](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_World_in_Data).[^cite_note-Deployment_of_COVID-19_vaccines_ourworldindata.org-309]

During [a pandemic](62750956.md) on the rapid timeline and scale of [COVID-19](63030231.md) cases in 2020, international organizations like the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) and [Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations](51522180.md) (CEPI), vaccine developers, governments, and industry evaluated the distribution of the eventual vaccine(s).[^cite_note-Deployment_of_COVID-19_vaccines_who-accel-310] Individual countries producing a vaccine may be persuaded to favor the highest bidder for manufacturing or provide first-class service to their own country.[^cite_note-Deployment_of_COVID-19_vaccines_gates1-311][^cite_note-Deployment_of_COVID-19_vaccines_hbr-312][^cite_note-Deployment_of_COVID-19_vaccines_reuters-313] Experts emphasize that licensed vaccines should be available and affordable for people at the frontlines of healthcare and in most need.[^cite_note-Deployment_of_COVID-19_vaccines_gates1-311][^cite_note-Deployment_of_COVID-19_vaccines_reuters-313]

In April 2020, it was reported that the UK agreed to work with 20 other countries and global organizations, including France, Germany, and Italy, to find a vaccine and share the results, and that UK citizens would not get preferential access to any new COVID‑19 vaccines developed by taxpayer-funded UK universities.[^cite_note-Deployment_of_COVID-19_vaccines_gartner-314] Several companies planned to initially manufacture a vaccine at [artificially low prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loss_leader), then increase prices for [profitability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Profit_\(economics\)) later if annual vaccinations are needed and as countries build stock for future needs.[^cite_note-Deployment_of_COVID-19_vaccines_reuters-313]

The WHO had set out the target to vaccinate 40% of the population of all countries by the end of 2021 and 70% by mid-2022,[^cite_note-315] but many countries missed the 40% target at the end of 2021.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317]

[Commons source](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File\:World%20map%20of%20share%20of%20people%20who%20received%20at%20least%20one%20dose%20of%20COVID-19%20vaccine%20by%20country.png).
